#2966. 2 Bargain Stocks to Buy With $1,000 in November

A drug maker and a food maker, both of which have attractive yields and strong businesses backing their dividends.

By Reuben Gregg Brewer – Nov 25, 2026 at 5:27PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Novo Nordisk ›

Novo Nordisk's (NVO +1.64%) yield is historically high at 4.4%. The same is true of PepsiCo's (PEP +0.88%) 4.1% yield. By comparison, the S&P 500 index's (^GSPC -0.25%) yield is a mere 1%. That suggests both are bargains as November draws to a close. Here's a closer look at why you should consider putting $1,000 into each one of these bargain stocks.

Novo Nordisk is a pharmaceutical company that is leaning into its Wegovy/Ozempic GLP-1 weight-loss portfolio. Wall Street is worried for two main reasons. First, it fumbled the ball after being the first to market with this new class of drugs. Second, it is shifting its business model to one driven by volume, which has required it to lower prices.

As to the first concern, Novo Nordisk's execution of its GLP-1 shot wasn't great, as it struggled to keep up with demand. It has been much better with its recent pill launch, which has seen dramatically higher demand. Moreover, there's likely room for more than one weight-loss drug given the size of the addressable market, so falling into second place in the GLP-1 space isnt the end of the world. Meanwhile, lowering prices has, indeed, hurt revenues and earnings. But the company upped its full-year 2026 guidance when it reported third-quarter 2026 earnings. That suggests that the volume-led transition is going well.

Novo Nordisk's 4.4% yield is historically high and well above the pharmaceutical industry average of 1.3%. Its roughly 10x price-to-earnings ratio is also well below the industry average of 27x. It looks like a bargain for more aggressive income investors, and a $1,000 investment will net you roughly 24 shares.

PepsiCo is one of the world's largest consumer staples companies. It is an industry leader in the snack and beverage areas, and it has a sizable packaged food business as well. It is more diversified than most of its peers, and it can stand toe-to-toe with any of them as a business, with strong brands, marketing, innovation, and distribution.

The problem is that PepsiCo's business isn't firing on all cylinders right now, with its organic sales growth notably lagging that of key peer Coca-Cola (KO -0.03%). Still, PepsiCo's 3.1% organic sales growth in the third quarter wasn't bad, even though it was less than half of Coca-Cola's 7%. But investors remain worried, thanks notably to weakness in PepsiCo's large U.S. operations.

That's fair, but PepsiCo is a Dividend King, with over 50 annual dividend increases. It has been through difficult times before and survived. And it is making the types of decisions you would expect during the current difficult period, including acquiring desirable brands and introducing innovative new products. It is highly likely that the company will get through this and thrive again over the long term.

If you buy it now, however, you get to collect the historically high 4.1% yield. And you are buying it while its 18x P/E is notably below its 25x five-year average. For reference, the average consumer staples stock yields 2% and has a P/E of around 26x. A $1k investment will buy roughly seven PepsiCo shares.

To be fair, neither Novo Nordisk nor PepsiCo is about to suddenly break out of their funk. So you have to go in with a long-term mindset. However, you'll get to collect a well above-market income stream while you wait for these historically well-run businesses to get back on track. And given their recent performance and historical results, it seems highly likely that they will eventually do just that.

#2965. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2964. Deere: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Deere's 2027 delivery plan hinges on flexible production schedules and labor contract talks, risks investors need to consider.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 25, 2026 at 5:38PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Deere & Company ›

On its fourth-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 25, 2026, Deere (DE +4.44%) announced it believes the global agricultural equipment cycle troughed in 2026, setting up a North American-led demand recovery for fiscal year 2027. Long-term investors should care because Deere sustained 12.2% full-year equipment operating margins during a sub-trough demand environment, demonstrating far stronger earnings power than in past downturns. With dealer inventories tight and aging fleets driving replacement demand, the business remains poised to compound returns as market volumes normalize. Three things management said stand out.

North American agricultural equipment demand is rebounding as aging machinery and depleted dealer lots force farmers to replace equipment. This structural replacement cycle underpins growth even while high input costs keep farm margins tight.

Deere proved its structural profitability by delivering over 12% operating margins in Equipment Operations (Equip Ops), even while its core Production & Precision Ag (PPA) segment operated below trough levels. Disciplined cost management and inventory controls kept margins well above those of previous industry downcycles.

Deere is expanding its digital tech stack into artificial intelligence through tools like JD, which connect to its Operations Center platform. Rapid adoption across 530 million engaged acres creates recurring software value and deepens customer lock-in.

Infrastructure spending and tight dealer fleets position construction equipment for upside as non-residential projects proceed. Structural cash generation provides flexibility to invest through industry cycles while acquiring complementary technologies.

Management is maintaining flexible production build schedules and preparing contingencies for upcoming labor contract negotiations to ensure continuity of customer deliveries. Structural precision tech integration positions the business for expanding profitability as demand recovers.

#2963. The Nvidia Scandal Set Off a Chip Crackdown in Congress. Which Stocks Are Most at Risk?

Congress is moving toward sweeping new chip export controls. One research firm says the biggest loser may not be an American company at all.

By Jonathan Ponciano – Nov 25, 2026 at 5:58PM EST

Nvidia (NVDA -3.91%) has spent November at the center of a political storm, with Jensen Huang reinstated as CEO amid allegations that its chips were smuggled into China. The stock fell another 4% on Wednesday. But the company most at risk from Washington's response may be one most investors don't connect to the scandal at all.

In a note published Wednesday, research firm TS Lombard said the Nvidia fallout has galvanized lawmakers, putting Congress on track to pass three to four new chip export-control measures, most likely as part of the fiscal 2027 National Defense Authorization Act. Grace Fan, the firm's managing director of global policy and disruptive themes research, said passage is a question of "when" and not "if," though a government shutdown fight around the Dec. 11 funding deadline could push it into early 2027.

The bills would close several loopholes at once. One would give Congress a say over exports of the most advanced AI chips. Another would require location tracking on exported AI chips to stop smuggling. A third would target Chinese companies renting U.S. chips through overseas cloud services. The centerpiece, the MATCH Act, goes after the machines that make chips rather than the chips themselves.

The MATCH Act would pressure U.S. allies, mainly the Netherlands and Japan, to match U.S. restrictions on the sale of chipmaking equipment to China. If they don't, TS Lombard said, the Senate version would direct the Commerce Department to impose the controls itself after about 150 days. The Senate bill also names every deep ultraviolet (DUV) lithography machine among the tools that couldn't go to China at all.

That's why TS Lombard says the companies most at risk aren't U.S. equipment makers such as Applied Materials (AMAT -3.89%), Lam Research (LRCX -5.68%), and KLA (KLAC +0.41%), which already face U.S. rules. Instead, it points to ASML (ASML -2.47%), the Dutch lithography leader, and Japan's Tokyo Electron, along with several other Japanese suppliers. The Dutch government has said it opposes the bill.

Wednesday's trading didn't reflect that ranking. Lam fell 6% and Applied Materials 4%, while ASML dropped 2.5% to about $1,303, roughly 35% below its 52-week high.

There are two reasons ASML's exposure is shrinking. TS Lombard estimates that China will account for about 20% of ASML's sales this year, down from roughly 30% in fiscal 2025. Chinese chipmakers also front-loaded their purchases, building what the firm estimates is about three years' worth of DUV machines.

That stockpile cuts both ways. It means fewer new China orders were coming anyway, but it shifts the fight to servicing and spare parts, which the Senate version would also restrict. TS Lombard expects China to retaliate in measured ways, possibly by squeezing Dutch and Japanese companies, and sees a likely middle path in which some servicing licenses are traded for access to critical minerals.

For long-term investors, yes, if you buy gradually. ASML is the only company that makes the extreme ultraviolet (EUV) machines needed for the most advanced chips, and that position doesn't depend on China. A smaller China business is a hit to near-term sales, not to its long-term moat.

The near-term risk is real, though. Headlines on the defense bill, the Dec. 11 funding deadline, and the Trump-Xi meeting at the G20 summit in Miami on Dec. 14-15 could all move the shares. Investors who want exposure to semiconductor stocks can use that volatility to build a position in stages rather than all at once, and keep an eye on Tokyo Electron and other tech stocks with heavy China exposure as the bill takes shape.

#2961. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2960. How to Get Grants for College

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Grants don’t have to be repaid, but the money can only go toward school-related costs. Here’s how to use grants to pay for school and how to make them go further.

Grants are one of the best ways you can pay for your education. It’s free money you aren’t expected to pay back.

Grants can be used for tuition, course materials and other cost-of-attendance expenses. Some grants, depending on who awards them, require an application or have requirements you must meet before you can get your money.

Here’s how to use a college grant and what it can pay for.

Federal grants come from the federal government.

State grants come from state programs and often can only be used at schools in that state.

Institutional grants come from your college or university, or even your program or major.

Private grants come from nonprofits, community organizations and individual donors.

To receive a federal grant, you must complete the Free Application for Federal Student Aid (FAFSA). Most states require the FAFSA for state grants, and a few require a separate state application. You’ll need to complete the FAFSA for each year you’re enrolled in school. You may need to meet certain eligibility requirements, such as income limits, to get some grants. The FAFSA is how you apply for the Pell Grant, which is a financial need-based grant. The FAFSA also determines eligibility for federal work-study, which is a job program rather than a grant.

Federal aid, including federal grants, typically requires students to meet satisfactory academic progress. This means you have to maintain a minimum grade point average and be on track to finish your degree on time, as determined by your school. Your school also sets how many credits you must take to qualify for grant funding.

Some grants — typically private or institutional grants — require an additional application that asks for transcripts, letters of recommendation and personal essays. Your school’s financial aid office or the grant provider will have details on applying for specific grants.

After you submit the FAFSA, you’ll receive a financial aid award letter from each school you applied to, which outlines how much in federal, state and institutional grants you’ve been offered. You must accept your aid to receive it.

Most grants will be disbursed directly to your college or university. Your school will apply the grant money first to tuition, fees and any other charges it bills, such as housing or a meal plan.

In some cases, you may receive more grant money than your school bills, which is the best-case scenario. If that happens to you, any leftover funds will likely be refunded to you. Refund timelines vary by school, so it may take some time to get your money. You can get your refund faster by setting up direct deposit with your school. You can then use the funds for other costs approved by your grant — some examples are detailed below.

If you have questions about your grant money, contact your college’s financial aid office.

Grants can help cover cost-of-attendance expenses. Generally, you can use grant money to pay for tuition, textbooks, class materials, transportation, housing and food. Depending on your school’s policies, you can use grant money to cover summer classes or study abroad programs, too.

Some grants come with specific terms about how you can spend them. Check your financial aid award letter or, for a private or institutional grant, the school’s or organization’s website to find specifics.

After your grant is applied to your tuition and school expenses, you can options to fill the gaps between what the grant covers and the total cost of your program. Some options include savings, scholarships, work-study, payment plans, federal student loans or, as a last resort, private loans. By using grants to pay for your college education, you may be able to borrow less.

To maximize the grants you’re eligible to receive, enter accurate information on the FAFSA and complete it as soon as you can — some funds are first-come, first-served. Also, don’t limit yourself to federal or state grants — search and apply for as many privately funded grants as you can find.

Your grant money usually goes straight to your college or university to pay your tuition and fees. Anything that is left over may be disbursed to you for other school-related costs.

Yes, but it depends on your grant. Housing is part of your cost of attendance, so you can use grant money to pay for off-campus rent.

You don’t have to pay back a grant as long as you continue to meet all requirements for the grant, such as staying enrolled. You can use grant money on school and school-related expenses. Some grants may require you to meet eligibility requirements such as a minimum GPA or state residency. If you receive more grant money than your cost of attendance for a given school term, ask your financial aid office if you can put that grant money toward next year’s cost of attendance.

If you have a federal grant like a Pell Grant, these are examples when you may have to repay all or part of the grant:

You received enough scholarship money to reduce your federal aid need.

It depends. You can usually use grant money to buy a laptop. Computers are considered course materials, which fall under cost-of-attendance expenses, so they can be covered by a grant. However, always double-check with the grant provider and your financial aid award letter to ensure that you’re following all restrictions. Whether you can use grant money to cover a laptop depends on your school’s policies and how much grant money you receive.

If you get more grant money than your school bills you, you’ll likely receive the leftover money to spend on other cost-of-attendance expenses. You can use this money on living costs such as transportation or housing. Grant money often goes mostly toward tuition, so a refund isn’t guaranteed.

Grant and scholarship money is generally tax-free when you use it for tuition, required fees, books and required class supplies. If you have additional questions, consider talking to a tax professional.

#2959. 'Big Short' Investor Michael Burry Just Closed His Bet Against the Nasdaq. Here's What Investors Should Do Now

The investor famous for calling the 2008 housing crash says he's now "temporarily almost entirely long." Here's how to read the move without copying it.

By Jonathan Ponciano – Nov 25, 2026 at 6:00PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Palantir Technologies ›

Michael Burry made his name betting against the housing market before the 2008 financial crisis. This week, with tech stocks in a steep slide, he did something somewhat unusual for him: He stopped betting against the market.

In a post on his Substack, Cassandra Unchained, Burry said he had sold the rest of his put options on the Nasdaq-100, bearish bets that pay off when the index falls. "Globally, my portfolio is more long, and less short, than it has been in some time," he wrote. Apart from puts on Palantir Technologies (PLTR +1.50%) and MetLife (MET +0.57%), he added, "I am temporarily almost entirely long."

Michael Burry. Image source: Getty Images.

Burry is turning more bullish after a sharp drop. The Nasdaq Composite closed Tuesday at 23,733.54, down 14.5% from its Oct. 6 close, and the Nasdaq-100 fell 16% over the same stretch. The Composite is down about 7% in November alone, as doubts about whether the AI spending boom will pay off, new tariff proposals, and the turmoil around Nvidia's leadership have weighed on AI stocks.

Puts gain value as an index falls, so selling them after a slide is a way to lock in profits. Burry said much of his cash, about 25% of the portfolio, came from profitable put options plus money added to the account.

The most important word in his post may be "temporarily." Burry said he is "continuing to assess short opportunities," and he hasn't said the market has bottomed. He also kept his Palantir puts, even though Palantir shares, at about $164 on Wednesday, already sit roughly 23% below their 52-week high.

Burry disclosed large put positions on Nvidia and Palantir in late 2025 after warning about an AI bubble. Shifting from that stance to mostly long tells you he thinks a good part of the downside he expected has now played out, at least for the broad index.

For most investors, the answer is not to copy his trades. Burry can reverse a position in a day and write about it afterward, so by the time a move becomes news, he may have changed it again. Short-selling and options are tools most individual investors shouldn't rely on.

The useful lesson is in how he's behaving. He took profits on a bet that worked, he's holding a big cash cushion, and he's staying selective about what he owns. You can do a version of that: Make a list of quality tech stocks you'd be glad to own for five years, and buy them in stages if the sell-off continues, rather than all at once. Keep enough cash on the side that you're never forced to sell in a downturn.

Markets are closed Thursday for Thanksgiving and close early on Friday, so expect thin trading and bigger swings to end the week. If Burry's right that the worst of the Nasdaq's slide is behind it, patient buyers will be rewarded. If he's wrong, he's already told you he can switch back, and investors buying in stages will have room to do the same.

#2958. Mortgage Rates Today, Wednesday, November 25: A Little Lower

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Rising inflation could, counterintuitively, help push mortgage rates further down.

Mortgage rates are a little lower this morning, and where they'll go next isn't totally clear.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage ticked down to 7.05% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is eight basis points lower than yesterday and 33 basis points lower than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Inflation data out this morning looks, at first glance, like bad news for mortgage rates. But depending on how markets react, we could actually see lower mortgage rates. Keep reading after the charts for all the details.

Below are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for perspective.

P.S. Markets will be closed tomorrow for Thanksgiving, and Black Friday's a half day, and mortgage rates don't move much when the markets aren't moving. I won't be here to talk to you about mortgage rates, either, 'cause NerdWallet gives us Thursday and Friday off. Happy Thanksgiving!

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Like I mentioned above, it's not totally obvious where mortgage rates will head next. Let's walk through the two scenarios.

Here's why rates could go up: This morning, we got October's Personal Consumption Expenditures price index, also known as PCE. It's the last measure of inflation to come out each month, but it's also the Federal Reserve's preferred metric, so PCE gets a lot of attention.

The stats came in mostly as predicted, though it's not like the predictions were good — inflation's been overly strong for a while. But the core year-over-year figure, which came in a tenth of a percent over predictions, could prove troublesome. Core PCE removes categories that can be extra volatile, so it's considered a measure that shows the underlying trend. In this case, the underlying trend is Not Good.

It's already pretty much a lock that we'll see a rate hike at the Federal Reserve's December meeting, because yes, raising the federal funds rate is the Fed's main tool for taming inflation. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates, but a rate-hiking cycle from the central bankers definitely keeps upward pressure on borrowing costs.

On the other hand, here's why that same inflation data could help rates go down: Over the past several days, we've watched the stock market go on a wild ride, with the Anthropic IPO and the return of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang. Yesterday, things weren't looking good for tech stocks, and these inflation numbers could make it even worse.

The amount that tech companies have borrowed to fund AI buildout is a significant concern for investors, since companies will have to cover those costs before investors see any profits. It's already a big task, and if everything costs more thanks to inflation, it's that much harder.

This AI-related anxiety is rough on the stock market, but it's been a boon for Treasury bonds. More demand for bonds pushes up bond prices, which brings down bond yields (basically, the return investors get on a bond). Mortgage rates for 30-year loans are generally benchmarked to the yield on the 10-year Treasury note, so here's why you care about all of this: Lower Treasury yields means lower mortgage rates, too. If investors keep stressing about inflation eating into tech profits, we could see mortgage rates continue to move lower.

That was a lot of info on inflation, but inflation's not the only major metric influencing rates.

Let's back up for a second. The Federal Reserve tries to keep the economy steady by focusing on two key goals, often referred to as the Fed's dual mandate. One side is price stability — keeping inflation reasonable. The other is maximum employment, meaning those who want to work are able to find jobs.

Next week we'll shift our focus to the employment side of the Federal Reserve's mandate, because the first week of the month is always all about employment data. On Tuesday, Dec. 1, we'll kick things off with the Job Openings and Labor Turnover Survey (JOLTS). JOLTS can tip us off to trends in the labor market; stuff like whether folks are leaving jobs voluntarily or if companies have openings that are going unfilled.

On Wednesday, payroll firm ADP will release its monthly employment stats. ADP is looking at its own payroll data to get an idea of how the job market is growing or contracting. The numbers don't include the public sector, but ADP often provides a preview of how the jobs report will go.

The Employment Situation Summary, better known as the jobs report, drops on Friday. This is the big one, since it tells us the nation's unemployment rate. The jobs report's other major stat is, similar to ADP, how many jobs the economy added or lost in the previous month. Unlike ADP though, the jobs report is inclusive of everybody in the public and private sectors.

Lately the labor market's been looking super healthy, which is great news for the economy but less positive for mortgage rates. When hiring's strong, the Fed can feel safer raising the federal funds rate (that's the short-term borrowing rate the central bankers adjust). Higher rates tend to discourage businesses from borrowing and expanding, so if the job market's on shaky footing, the central bankers are going to be cautious about raising the benchmark rate.

What we're seeing now though, where inflation is clearly the problem and employment's just fine, is a recipe for higher rates. Again, the Fed doesn't control mortgage rates, but the central bank being in rate-hike mode puts upward pressure on mortgage rates.

If we were to see employment falter in this latest round of numbers, it still probably wouldn't change the Fed's plan. That would be one poor month for the labor market versus months upon months of inflation running rampant — so yeah, they'll keep an eye on jobs, but inflation's still the scourge.

Our most likely source of lower mortgage rates in the short term is what I described above; investors who are leery of inflation and the promise of AI looking to the safe harbor of the bond market.

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you could start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.55% or higher — but there just aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

#2957. If SpaceX and Tesla Merge Next Year, Here's What a $5,000 Investment in the Combined Company Could Be Worth by the End of 2028

An investment in Tesla could double in the event of a Space Exploration Technologies merger.

By Geoffrey Seiler – Nov 25, 2026 at 6:04PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Space Exploration Technologies ›

The odds of Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +1.30%) and Tesla (TSLA -1.18%) merging sometime next year have been growing. Prediction market odds jumped earlier this month after Elon Musk's biographer, Walter Isaacson, told CNBC he was confident a merger is coming, since keeping the companies separate makes no sense. This was followed by the two companies picking board members to begin official merger talks.

Isaacson's comments raised the odds that SpaceX would announce a merger with Tesla before March 1, 2027, from 27% on Nov. 11, 2026, to 62% two days later. They now sit at 67%, as of Nov. 24 on Kalshi. Meanwhile, the odds of a merger announcement before the end of 2027 are at 89% on Polymarket.

Now, a merger isn't a foregone conclusion and does face some hurdles, but it does seem very likely at this point. The Democrats' sweeping victories in the midterm elections complicate matters a bit, as Texas Attorney General-elect Nathan Johnson told Bloomberg that a potential merger would face "tremendous scrutiny." Meanwhile, more than half of all Tesla shareholders would need to vote to approve the merger, not just those who decide to vote. Musk owns nearly 20% of the company, but Tesla also has a significant retail shareholder base that would have to vote on the deal for it to pass.

Despite these obstacles, there is still a very good shot that the companies announce a merger next year and complete it by the end of 2028. In such a scenario, it would be best to purchase shares of Tesla, the acquired company. With a little over $5,000, an investor could buy around 13 shares of Tesla.

An acquisition of Tesla by SpaceX ultimately makes a lot of sense. First, it unites Musk's two largest companies and will allow him to focus on a single vision instead of splitting his time between the two. Second, the companies already have an important joint venture in the form of their massive chip manufacturing facility, Terafab, so it would make sense to have this under one roof. Finally, the companies' businesses complement each other.

More than anything, both SpaceX and Tesla are about the future, and together they give Musk and investors more shots on goal. Meanwhile, SpaceX could ultimately need some of Tesla's technology to build its planned data centers in space, including solar and Optimus robots. As such, the deal makes sense from a synergy perspective, and the company would be able to cut some overlapping corporate costs.

Any merger between SpaceX and Tesla is likely to be an all-stock deal in which Tesla is acquired at a premium. Analysts at HSBC recently pegged the most likely premium around 33% based on past similar deals, which makes sense.

Based on that premium and current prices, investors would get a little over 4 SpaceX shares for each Tesla share they hold. That would turn investors' 13 Tesla shares into 52 SpaceX shares after the merger, worth $5,000.

For a combined SpaceX-Tesla, the stock would likely trade based on a forward price-to-sales (P/S) ratio. For 2029, the Tesla revenue consensus estimate is $146 billion, while SpaceX's is roughly $336 billion. Combined, that's $482 billion in estimated annual revenue for 2029.

Place a 12-times multiple on that, given its strong growth outlook, and the combined company would be worth $5.8 trillion. Meanwhile, following the merger, it would have roughly 29.7 billion shares. That would value SpaceX at approximately $195. Those 52 shares would then be worth about $10,150, approximately double a $5,000 initial investment in two years.

This makes Tesla a top growth stock to buy ahead of a potential SpaceX merger.

#2956. Why GDS Holdings Stock Topped the Market Today

The company posted a much narrower loss than many expected in its third quarter.

By Eric Volkman – Nov 25, 2026 at 6:27PM EST

10 stocks we like better than GDS Holdings ›

Tech stocks didn't do well in the last trading session before Thanksgiving. One exception was Chinese techie GDS Holdings (GDS +0.07%); its American Depositary Shares (ADSs) inched up marginally on a day when the S&P 500 index dipped by almost 0.3%. It managed to swim against the current due to its latest earnings release, which featured better-than-expected fundamentals.

GDS Holdings' third quarter saw the company reap 3.18 billion yuan ($477 million) in revenue, up 10% year over year. On the bottom line, the company flipped to a loss of 5.47 million yuan ($821 million) from the year-ago profit of 712.31 million yuan ($107 million) -- although the third-quarter 2025 figure was inflated by a one-time accounting gain of 1.37 billion yuan ($206 million). On a per-ADS basis, GDS' loss was 0.04 yuan ($0.01).

Although no investor appreciates a tumble into the red, analysts tracking GDS were expecting far worse. Their estimate for net loss was 0.24 yuan ($0.04) per ADS. The company also beat the average pundit projection of 3.14 billion yuan ($471 million) for revenue.

GDS is a company in transition, shifting from a cloud computing focus to a focus on operating artificial intelligence (AI) data centers. The company said new bookings and continued progress in getting its facilities onstream helped push revenue higher. Higher costs, primarily from data center build-outs, affected the bottom line.

In the earnings release, GDS reaffirmed its existing annual revenue and earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) guidance. The former line item should come in at 12.7 billion yuan ($1.91 billion) to 13 billion yuan ($1.95 billion), which forecasts at least 11% growth over the 2025 figure. Non-GAAP (adjusted) EBITDA is estimated at 5.9 billion yuan ($885 million) to 6.1 billion yuan ($915 million).

The current pullback in AI infrastructure stocks in the U.S. market weighed on sentiment toward GDS, whose quarterly performance was actually quite encouraging. If I were an ADS holder, I'd be pleased that revenue growth is poised to accelerate, given the company's backlog and considerable committed space across its facilities.

#2955. Ready for ‘The Great Lock In’? Viral Money Trend Can Improve Focus

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Winter is the ideal time to make progress on your financial goals.

When it’s cold outside, it’s the ideal time to stay home, organize your finances, set some new money goals and make progress toward them.

That’s the promise of the viral winter money trend known as “The Great Lock In” that’s circulating on social media.

“You’re probably not doing too much,” says Robert Baird, wealth manager at Cornerstone Financial Services in Southfield, Michigan. “It gets dark early. So those can be great months to lock into saving and not spend in excess.”

One important component of the Great Lock In is developing new routines that will last well into 2027, says Jordan Mangaliman, founder and fiduciary wealth advisor at GoldLine Wealth Management in Fullerton, California.

“We want a long-term habit versus a Band-Aid fix,” he says. “In today’s economy, money goes in and out so blindingly fast.”

Ready to join the movement? Here’s how to get started.

What’s your money moonshot? Maybe it’s paying off all your credit card debt or purchasing a home. Or spending less than you earn. Defining it is the first step toward achieving it.

“Spend a lot of time identifying what that goal is,” Baird says. “Everyone’s in a different position, so comparing yourself to what you see online might not be applicable.”

Even while setting ambitious money goals, it’s worth grounding them in reality, Mangaliman says.

If you want to save a larger portion of your income, he suggests trying the new saving pattern for a couple of months to see how it feels rather than saying you’ll do it forever and then getting frustrated.

“It’s a marathon, not a sprint,” Mangaliman adds.

Once you’ve outlined those big goals for your Great Lock In, create a budget to help you achieve them, Baird says.

He suggests combing through your previous credit or debit card spending to see where your money is going. Then you can decide what you want to change or shift. Using a tool like the 50/30/20 budget calculator can also help. This approach allots 50% of take-home pay toward needs, 30% toward wants and 20% toward savings and debt payments beyond the minimum.

Baird suggests including saving as a line item in your budget so you don’t forget to do it. “Building that cost into your budget will put you in a better position later,” he says.

Any Great Lock In should include growing your savings, because that makes achieving other goals easier, Baird says.

“Maybe there’s something you could cut in the short term to build up that savings,” he says. Good targets include subscription services, food delivery, entertainment and travel costs.

Saving more is especially challenging in today’s environment, when everyday expenses like gas are higher than they were a year ago.

Baird says that makes winter a good time to try to reduce costs. There can be fewer temptations to go out and spend money when the days are shorter and the temperatures are colder.

Mangaliman recommends setting aside three to six months’ worth of expenses, although even a smaller amount is helpful. “Ultimately it’s about what you can afford,” he says. “It’s the foundation of your financial house.”

Along with building savings, paying down high-interest debt is another pillar of the Great Lock In.

With credit card interest rates higher than they were a few years ago, carrying credit card debt has gotten more expensive. “I would consider applying some savings to paying down debt, especially right now when your debt is costing you more than you can earn in savings,” Baird says. (You still want to reserve an emergency fund to cover unexpected expenses.)

“High-interest debt is one of the biggest thieves of wealth,” Mangaliman says. And one way to avoid turning to debt again in the future is to build up that emergency fund so it’s there for you if you need it.

Even when you are committed to a Great Lock In, some hiccups along the way are inevitable. Success depends on continuing anyway.

“It’s like a diet,” Baird says. “Just because you ate ice cream on a Sunday doesn’t mean on Monday you can’t go right back to being disciplined again.”

“Be kind to yourself and do everything in moderation,” Mangaliman suggests. Just wanting to improve your finances is already a great first step, he says.

In fact, he embraces the Great Lock In mentality himself. “There’s an old saying: It’s not how you start, but how you finish,” he says. “And you want to finish the year on a strong note so you carry those habits into 2027 and beyond.”

#2954. Here’s How Many Coca-Cola Shares It Takes to Earn $1,300 Every Quarter in Dividends

You would need to own more than 2,450 shares of Coca-Cola to generate $1,300 in dividend income.

By Matthew Benjamin – Nov 25, 2026 at 6:28PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Coca-Cola ›

Coca-Cola (KO -0.03%) has been a great stock to own in 2026.

Shares of the giant beverage maker are up more than 40% year to date (compared with the S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.25%), which has risen just about 4.5% this year).

In addition to that impressive gain, Coca-Cola is also a Dividend King, a company that has raised its dividend for at least 50 consecutive years. In fact, Coke has raised its dividend for 64 consecutive years.

That annual dividend currently stands at $2.12 per share, or $0.53 each quarter.

So, to earn a healthy $1,300 per quarter in dividends, how many shares would you need to own? Well, it's as simple as dividing $1,300 by 0.53.

You would need to own 2,453 shares to generate that much quarterly dividend income. That's a hefty investment: That many shares would cost $240,394 at the current share price of $98.

All that said, I believe Coca-Cola is a very good investment right now for two main reasons. First, the stock has historically held up well in recessions and bear markets. And second, while the rise of GLP-1 drugs is hurting some snack food and beverage companies, about 68% of Coca-Cola's portfolio consists of low- or no-calorie drink options.

So, you might not be able to afford enough KO shares to generate over $1,000 in income per quarter, but an investment in this stock is, in my opinion, a wise one.

#2953. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

#2952. JFrog's 2027 Outlook: Strong Enterprise Adoption Signals a Widening Moat

This under-the-radar software infrastructure stock boasts a Superscore of 84 from our Hidden Gems Primary database, part of The Motley Fool's Moneyball Database system. Here's why.

By Robert Izquierdo – Updated Nov 25, 2026 at 6:29PM EST

When a large enterprise needs to update its software, it doesn't just push a button and hope for the best. It relies on a central nervous system to store, secure, and distribute those updates across thousands of servers. JFrog (FROG +0.27%) has built that system. By acting as the universal repository for software packages, it has become the invisible plumbing that powers global application delivery. The stock, which trades at $109.60 as of November 19, 2026, has gained 80% over the past year as developers increasingly lean on its platform to manage the complexity of modern, AI-integrated software supply chains.

Our proprietary Hidden Gems scoring system assigns JFrog an overall Superscore of 84 out of 100, placing it in the Strong category. The Superscore is an AI-powered score that evaluates a company's overall strength by combining financial performance, product market position, technological capabilities, leadership quality, and relative valuation. It represents the unification of all our scores into a single score for public companies, with five rating bands: Exceptional (90-100), Strong (75-89), Above Average (60-74), Average (40-59), and Cautious (0-39).

A score of 84 places JFrog ahead of roughly 95 out of every 100 companies we score. This analysis pairs the business strengths driving that score with the risks that keep it from being higher, providing a research foundation for your own due diligence.

The Hidden Gems Superscore reflects The Motley Fool's proprietary AI-driven evaluation of a company across product, financial, leadership, and valuation pillars as of the article date and may change over time. Performance figures are point-in-time. Past performance does not guarantee future results.

You may want to keep researching before buying if...

The Superscore is one data-driven signal worth investigating, but it does not constitute a recommendation. Always weigh this data against your personal financial goals, risk tolerance, and individual research before making an investment decision.

JFrog is in a great position to grow its business over the next several years thanks to the artificial intelligence boom. AI is making it easier and faster for developers to generate code. As a result, enterprises need automated systems to store, track, and govern the resulting software packages and AI models.

This contributed to JFrog's enterprise customers signing massive, multi-year contracts resulting in a 60% year-over-year increase in Q3 cloud sales to $101.5 million. Cloud income represented 46% of revenue last year, and has grown to 57% at the end of the third quarter in a sign JFrog's enterprise moat is widening, anchored by high switching costs.

Another factor in JFrog's favor is that it bundles cybersecurity features into its platform, strengthening the safety and security of the software code it manages. This also delivers a unified "system of record" that increases contract sizes and platform stickiness, encouraging customers to consolidate their spending with JFrog.

The success of this approach is demonstrated by the company's robust net dollar retention rate of 124%, up from 118% in the previous year. As the use of AI code continues to expand, JFrog is poised for years of ongoing business expansion ahead.

#2951. How I Learned to Stop Worrying and Love the Wolves

Game 7 of the Western Conference Semifinals against the Sacramento Kings. My dad was in the family room, having what looked to be some kind of religious experience. 13-year-old me walked in, alarmed and… concerned? He had always been a bit of a curmudgeon, and now here he was, dancing in front of the TV, on the verge of bursting into a full-blown "Sister Act" number. I had never seen this side of him before. Whatever had my dad so enraptured seemed like something I'd like to be a part of myself.

"I'll have what he's having," essentially.

In that game, the Big Ticket, Kevin Garnett, would put up a godly stat line of 32 points, 21 boards, five blocks, and four steals, leading the Minnesota Timberwolves over the Kings with a final score of 83-80. It was pandemonium in the Dooley household.

May 19th, 2004, was the date that I was baptized and purified in the waters of Wolves basketball.

They'd go on to play the Los Angeles Lakers in the Western Conference Finals, and all I really remember from that series was Game 6, with the Wolves down 2-3. My dad and I drove over to my grandma's house off Lake Nokomis in South Minneapolis for a Monday visit and to anxiously watch the most important game of the season.

In my grandma's living room, I sank helplessly in her old reclining sofa chair as Kareem "not Abdul-Jabbar" Rush came off the bench and unconsciously fired away haymaker after haymaker from deep. The man they called K-Rush would hit his first six threes before finally missing late in the fourth when the damage had already been done.

In just six games, the Wolves were sent packing by a relative no-name on a roster full of future Hall of Famers. As someone new to the sport, even I knew that this was almost certainly not on the scouting report. Little did I know at the time, though, that the K-Rushes of the league would turn out to be some of my favorite players. But that night at least, I found K-Rush to be a pretty big jerk.

After learning what it felt like to be punched in the gut by sports – and I must say, it didn't feel great – I was now impatiently waiting for the next season to kick off.

Actually, I was more than ready. The Wolves would be ready as well. I always loved a good story, and the Wolves' was a vengeance quest. They would return. And this time around, they would end up hoisting the trophy that, two weeks later, after the Detroit Pistons shellacked those no-good-dirty-rotten Lakers, I learned belonged to some dude named Larry O'Brien, former NBA commissioner and politician who, according to his Wikipedia page, "learned about politics at a young age."

That hardware was on my mind for the rest of the summer. "Hey, Patrick, wanna go to Sebastian Joe's with me and Sam?" Sorry, can't. Too busy thinking about the Larry O'Brien Trophy.

A female classmate of mine asked me if I wanted to be her boyfriend. After I had just learned about this huge summer event called the NBA draft? Sorry, Ali, I gotta study.

Before I knew it, summer had ended, fall was upon us, and the 2004-05 season was just around the corner. My first full season as a fan of the Minnesota Timberwolves.

The Minnesota Timberwolves join terrifying kids commercials as some of my worst nightmare fuel.

Your teenage years are hard enough as it is. I didn't make it any easier for myself.

I tuned into every game that '04-05 season, waiting for the good vibes to come, but they never did. The January 19th game in Los Angeles against the antagonistic Lakers was an especially nauseating experience. I lay in bed trying to multitask between half-sleeping and listening to Chad Hartman on the radio call. The lights remained on deep into the night. The game was a back-and-forth tilt, with neither team able to get any real separation.

In the end, though, my AM radio would taunt me with Hartman's defeated utterances underneath the static scratches, of missed shots on one end and made shots on the other. Neither were from the right team. It felt unjust. What did I do to deserve this?

Despite running it back, the Wolves just couldn't get over the hump that year, and by proxy, I felt like I couldn't either. This thing called "Wolves fandom" had me feeling sluggish and languid. Like that gut punch from Game 6 of the WCF, I was learning in real time what prolonged, sustained depression feels like. Every game had me on pins and needles. I'm pretty sure this was the year I developed insomnia. I started having frequent sleep paralysis episodes, and also some of the most bizarre nightmares you could imagine.

My dad and I attended the Wolves' final regular-season game that year against the San Antonio Spurs, a team that two months later would be crowned NBA champions. In perhaps their best showing of the season, the Wolves dominated, winning by 22 points.

Former Wolves project Ndudi Ebi had what, to this day, is still the worst airball I have ever seen on a three-point "attempt". He also brought the roof down with a ferocious dunk that had the whole backboard shaking. That was the joy of guys like that.

We were sitting in the lower level right behind the hoop, equal parts in awe and also concerned that we were about to bear witness to a Shaq-shattering-the-backboard moment. (Un)fortunately, that moment never came, and after the game had ended, we left the Target Center wondering what could have been. If they had just played like that all season, maybe things could've gone differently.

Trendy pre-season favorites to win it all, the Wolves missed the playoffs that year, only managing to go 44-38. The late great coach Flip Saunders was fired 51 games into the season, replaced by General Manager and very tall man Kevin McHale.

Little did I know that, over the next 14 years, when my age would double and my stubble would fully grow into a lumberjack beard, at least record-wise, 2005 would be the summit.

The years that followed were a fast, rocky descent down a black diamond, and I was a kid with two left skis, held together with duct tape and blind hope.

Wavering optimism in the circles of Hell, or the life of a Wolves fan.

Sometimes I felt I had made a terrible mistake.

I had unknowingly chained a handcuff I didn't even know I was wearing to a ball dribbled by jesters, malcontents, and egocentrics: Ricky Davis, Marko Jarić, future podcaster Rashad McCants, and some guy with two first names whose scoring was inexplicably halved (20.3 to 10.1) because, as I was starting to realize, the Wolves were a team that could never have nice things.

Dwane Casey was hired as head coach for the 2005-06 season, and then was disposed of a season and a half later. This team was a steaming pile of dung, and the joke was on me for locking myself in the barn, waiting for the heap to turn to roses.

Fast-forward to the summer of '08: "The Dark Knight" had just come out. I went to the Southdale theater to watch the blockbuster film my peers were touting as "the best movie ever", a performance by the late Heath Ledger that critics were lauding as "Oscar-worthy". They weren't wrong. It was quite transformative. I thought to myself, "This was the same guy who played that cowboy?"

Now, I never contemplated becoming the Joker. If it had been released a year earlier, however, I may have considered it.

The previous summer, Garnett had finally had enough and rather unceremoniously parted ways with the Wolves in a big "blockbuster" trade. Minnesota's return really only had two bright spots to note, the first obviously being Al "Big Al" Jefferson.

Defending dunk contest champion Gerald Green.

Under the tutelage of perpetually confused Randy Wittman, the 2007-08 Wolves would go 22-60. Make no mistake, they were god-awful. But during that season's all-star game festivities, something finally clicked for me.

At New Orleans Arena, Gerald Green participated in the Saturday night dunk contest to defend his title, ultimately going toe-to-toe with Dwight Howard, AKA "Superman". Green broke out all sorts of crazy dunks: the alley-oop windmill, a behind-the-backboard under-the-legs slam. But there was one dunk that I will never, ever forget.

The cupcake dunk had never been pulled off, let alone even attempted. It has still not been replicated, nor has anyone, to my knowledge, dared even try.

Though Green would go on to relinquish his title to Howard, that dunk was the talk of the night. And I truly believe the only reason he didn't get a perfect score on it was because the judges didn't see what we all saw.

And it got me thinking. On a rudderless team, both managed and saddled by an inept franchise, what else is there to see? What can I look for?

To start, I accepted the truth: this team wasn't winning anything anytime soon.

Now that actual expectations had been tabled, I could watch the Wolves without worrying about superfluous things like "the outcome of a game" or "where the Wolves sit in the standings". If they lost, well, that's okay. I expected them to. If they won, I was amused. I had even perfected my dad's patented half-smile.

I also became a more well-rounded basketball fan, always following a second team to supplement my thirst for promising development. After the Seattle SuperSonics moved to Oklahoma City in 2008, I started to follow the young upstart Thunder. Making the leap from 23 wins to 50 in just one year. As a Wolves fan, I could've felt envy, but instead I was like a proud stepfather. I was the fan that stepped up.

In 2012, when they made it all the way to the Finals only to lose to that villainous Miami Heat superteam, I took it personally. But it felt good to care. The stakes had never been higher. I could never feel that way about the Wolves, who were in a league of their own.

And I tried to see it all for what it was: just basketball. Like the Actor in Albert Camus' "The Myth of Sisyphus", I invested myself in the Wolves for two-ish hours every game night, and once the contest was over, it was back to business as usual. It was back to life as usual. I wouldn't let the Wolves define me. They wouldn't determine how I felt moving forward. Sometimes a game would end in heartbreaking fashion, and I would feel that tiny hammer starting to peck away at that thing in my chest. No. I would relent. I refuse to let it hurt.

Well, if I was built like this guy, this would have been way easier.

But eventually I had to interrogate something deep: Why did I start following this team in the first place?

I had chosen to attach myself to this team during some of the most formative years of my life. And despite the Wolves at times almost insisting that I leave them be, I could never.

My father has lived in Minneapolis his entire life. As have I. I guess you could say we are predisposed to Minnesota sports fandom. It's in our bloodstream. When I watched my father react so fervently to that epic Game 7 all those years ago, it felt like something I would like to share with him. Up to that point, we hadn't shared all that much.

That shared fandom extends far beyond the father-son bond. It's a communal thing. Minneapolis is Wolves territory. To have pride in them is just one of the ways I choose to have pride in my community, where I'm from and where I stay to this day.

In 2013, having just graduated from the University of St. Thomas, I discovered Canis Hoopus, another communal outlet for me to share my allegiance to Wolves basketball. That became a way for me to lean into the real truth I had been avoiding. Like it or not, the Wolves were now an integral part of me. And to ignore that part, to detach myself from it and let myself become jaded would, as Camus so bluntly put it, be a defeat of the human soul.

So, whether I was using Canis to spam the game threads with Free Dieng! or as an excuse to play around with iMovie, I was no longer afraid to let it all out. The Wolves were like an alley-oop, and I was Gerald Green, or Zach LaVine – take your pick; if there's one thing the Wolves have, it's dunk champs – and the team was all of us, together.

Not too long after Garnett was traded, another movie came out: "Into the Wild", adapted from the novel of the same name and the real-life story of Christopher McCandless, a naive, pretentious young college graduate who, left to his own devices, went on a solo hike across North America and into the Alaskan wilderness. Along the way, he made many friends, but ultimately always left them behind to continue his trek alone.

Eventually, he ate a berry that turned out to be poisonous. He would soon pass away in an abandoned bus in the wild, but not before writing in his journal a revelatory "Happiness is only real when shared."

While I find that a bit reductive, I believe that happiness is best when shared. And it's not just limited to happiness. As my former neighbor Dave Pirner once sang, "Misery loves company."

The highs and the lows, the celebratory and the funereal. All of it is best, and can be eased, by a shared camaraderie among people.

And that's how I learned to truly embrace the Wolves, by finding community in the struggle, and by finding myself in that communal openness where all mirrors are welcome, where the subjective woe can turn into a collective joy, and I am forever grateful for that.
